Short answer: you’re in good shape. The math I can see is consistent; the structure is just a bit atomized and there are duplicate / legacy files. I’ll do three things:

Propose a 20‑file canonical layout (which files to keep, which to merge, which to retire).

Give targeted rigor + math comments on the key analytic and YM sections.

Sketch how to mechanically apply the re-org.

1. Canonical ~20‑file structure

Right now you effectively have:

Part I: sections 1–5, with some older vs UPDATED variants.

Part II: sections 1, 1B, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, again with duplicates.

Part III: sections 1–6, some with UPDATED versions.

Part IV: sections 1–4.

Part V: section 5.

We can hit your “~20 files” target by:

Picking a single canonical version for each section (always the UPDATED / (1) / FULL one), and

Merging a few small “scope/summary” sections into their main neighbors.

Here’s a concrete mapping you can implement.

2.6. Stability under finite products

The Bakry–Émery tensor is additive under products, and so is the curvature bound: finite products of single-site systems preserve the same curvature constant 
𝜌
ρ. This is essential for passing from a single-site or single-plaquette model to a full finite lattice by taking finite products of configuration spaces.

5.5. Summary — Position of Part 1 in the Proof Chain

Part 1 has built a general curvature-controlled diffusion framework:

Inputs:

The scalar

The abstract Yang–Mills interface clarifies what needs to be proved in the gauge-theoretic setting: a horizontal Bakry–Émery curvature bound (local in field space, uniform in volume) feeding into the same analytic engine.

In Part 2, we build the lattice Yang–Mills configuration manifold, analyze the Wilson action and Haar curvature, and prove the local horizontal curvature hypotheses that will plug directly into the machinery assembled here.

Part 1. Global Strategy and Geometric Framework

Role: Set the stage for the entire program: what “curvature-controlled Gibbs measures” are, what the endgame (Yang–Mills mass gap) is, and how the geometric/analytic modules fit.

Content to merge:

What this part proves/explains:
	•	General setup: finite-dimensional Riemannian manifolds, Gibbs measures, generators, carré du champ.
	•	What “curvature lower bounds ⇒ spectral gap” means.
	•	How the program breaks into: curvature (Part I), lattice YM geometry (Part II), local→global FI (Part III), OS/Hamiltonian/continuum (Part IV–V).

	•	The CD(ρ,∞) condition and its equivalent formulations in this finite-dimensional setting.
	•	Product stability and basic examples (e.g. Gaussian).

This is where the “curvature-controlled” in your title gets its analytic teeth.

This part is your “black box” you later feed the YM geometry into.

Part 5. Abstract Interface to Gauge-Invariant Theories

Role: Abstract the gauge geometry into the Part I machine: vertical/horizontal splitting, gauge-invariant observables, and horizontal curvature hypotheses.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_I_SECTION_5_Abstract_Interface_YM.md
	•	PART_I_SECTION_5_Abstract_Interface_YM(1).md (just reconcile, choose UPDATED material as primary).  

Main results:
	•	Abstract family (M_\Lambda,g_\Lambda,S_\Lambda) with gauge group \mathcal{G}_\Lambda acting isometrically.  
	•	Vertical spaces (gauge orbits) V_x, horizontal spaces H_x = V_x^\perp.
	•	Gauge-invariant observables f: \nabla f(x)\in H_x, so only horizontal curvature matters.  
	•	Hypothesis H_{\text{curv}}: uniform horizontal BE curvature lower bound ⇒ uniform Poincaré/LSI in the gauge-invariant sector.  

This is exactly the interface you plug Part II’s concrete YM geometry into.

⸻

Part 6. Lattice Configuration Manifold, Haar Geometry, and Ricci

Role: Concrete geometry of lattice YM: what the finite-volume configuration space looks like and what its curvature is before adding the action.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_1_Lattice_Configuration_Manifold_and_Metric.md
	•	Part_II_Section_1_UPDATED.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_5_Haar_Measure_and_Haar_Mass(1).md and PART_II_SECTION_5_Haar_Measure_and_Haar_Mass(1)(1).md.  

Main results:
	•	M_\Lambda = G^{E(\Lambda)}, product bi-invariant metric g_\Lambda, product Haar volume.  
	•	Product Ricci: \mathrm{Ric}_{g_\Lambda} = \kappa_G g_\Lambda with \kappa_G>0 independent of \Lambda.  
	•	Exponential coordinates and Haar “mass term” as a strictly convex effective potential near the identity.  

This part is the geometric backbone: you show the group itself is positively curved and that this produces a uniform mass-like term.

⸻

Part 7. Wilson Action, Gauge Symmetry, and Reflection Positivity (Configuration Level)

Role: Put the Wilson action on the manifold, define the gauge group action, and record reflection positivity of the action (as distinct from OS reflection positivity of the full measure).

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_2_Wilson_Action_and_Gauge_Invariance.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_2_FULL_Wilson_and_RP.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_1B_Reflection_Positivity_of_Wilson_Action.md
	•	If needed, bits of PART_II_SECTION_2_Wilson_Action_and_Gauge_Invariance(1).md.

Main results:
	•	Definition of plaquette holonomies and Wilson plaquette action S_W.  
	•	Lattice gauge group \mathcal{G}_\Lambda acting by endpoint conjugation; action is isometric and preserves volume.  
	•	Gauge invariance of S_W and definition of the Wilson Gibbs measure.
	•	(In this chapter you can already foreshadow reflection positivity at the action level; full OS reflection positivity gets treated later.)

⸻

Part 8. Gauge Orbits, Vertical/Horizontal Decomposition, and the Physical Sector

Role: Implement the abstract interface of Part 5 in the concrete lattice YM setting.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_3_Gauge_Orbits_and_Horizontal_Subspaces.md
	•	Any tiny overlap with the abstract interface in Part 5 can be cross-referenced rather than duplicated.  

Main results:
	•	Gauge orbits \mathcal{O}_U, regular configurations, vertical spaces V_U = T_U \mathcal{O}_U.  
	•	Horizontal spaces H_U = V_U^\perp, gauge-equivariance.
	•	Gauge-invariant observables have horizontal gradients: \nabla f(U)\in H_U.  
	•	Dirichlet form on gauge-invariant observables equals the Dirichlet form on the quotient M_\Lambda / \mathcal{G}_\Lambda.

So this part is: “here is the physical tangent bundle we will put curvature on.”

⸻

Part 9. Wilson Hessian, Discrete Hodge Decomposition, and Physical Positivity

Role: Analyze the second variation of the Wilson action and show how it acts on pure gauge, harmonic, and co-exact modes.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_4_Wilson_Hessian_and_Physical_Positivity.md
	•	Part_II_Section_4_UPDATED.md  

Main results:
	•	Identification of the Wilson Hessian with a discrete Laplacian: \nabla^2 S_W(U^{(0)}) = 2 c_W d_1^* d_1 in cochain language.  
	•	Decomposition into pure gauge, harmonic, and co-exact modes; Hessian is strictly positive on co-exact (physical) modes, vanishes on gauge directions.
	•	Combination with Haar mass (from Part 6) to see full positivity on horizontal directions at the vacuum.

This is the “linearized YM = discrete Maxwell + mass from Haar” chapter.

⸻

Part 10. Haar Mass, Effective Potential, and Local Horizontal Bakry–Émery Bound

Role: Put together the Haar curvature and the action Hessian into a local BE curvature lower bound on physical directions.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound(1).md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound_UPDATED.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_6_Local_Horizontal_Bakry_Emery_Bound_UPDATED(1).md
(Consolidate to the cleanest UPDATED version, supplement with any missing lemmas.)  

Main results:
	•	Expression \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_\Lambda} = \mathrm{Ric}_{g_\Lambda} + \nabla^2 S_\Lambda and its restriction to H_U.
	•	At U^{(0)}, horizontal curvature bound \mathrm{Ric}_{\mu_\Lambda}(U^{(0)})(v,v)\ge (\kappa_G - C_{\mathrm{add}})|v|^2 with \kappa_G>C_{\mathrm{add}}.
	•	By continuity, existence of a small-field radius r>0 and \rho_{\text{loc}}>0 so that this holds on a ball B_r(U^{(0)}), uniformly in \Lambda.  

This is the YM analogue of the scalar convexity bound: a local CD(ρ,∞) in physical directions near the trivial configuration.

Part 11. Core Curvature Theorem and Interface Back to Part I

Role: Package Part 6–10 into a single, clean theorem that exactly matches the abstract hypothesis from Part 5.

Content to merge:
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I_UPDATED.md
	•	PART_II_SECTION_7_Core_Curvature_Theorem_and_Interface_to_Part_I_UPDATED(1).md
	•	The “Technical Addendum” on horizontal BE curvature at the end.

Main results:
	•	Theorem: For effective action S_\Lambda = S_W + S_{\text{add},\Lambda} with local, gauge-invariant and Hessian-bounded S_{\text{add},\Lambda}, plus \mathrm{Ric}_G\ge\kappa_G g_G and \kappa_G>C_{\text{add}}, there exist \rho_{\text{loc}}>0,r>0 independent of \Lambda such that
	•	Horizontal BE curvature \ge \rho_{\text{loc}} on B_r(U^{(0)}),
	•	Hence local CD(\rho_{\text{loc}},\infty) for gauge-invariant observables.  
	•	Explicit restatement as hypothesis H_{\text{curv,loc}} for Part III.

This part is the “this is exactly the input the abstract machinery wants” chapter.

Content to merge:

	•	Theorem: global Poincaré inequality with explicit constant C_P depending only on \rho_{\text{loc}},\alpha,\beta,\mu(K).
	•	Interpretation in finite-volume context: this gives a volume-uniform spectral gap for the diffusion generator once you have a volume-uniform Lyapunov bound.

2.9. Interface with the rest of the program

The material of this part plays three distinct roles in the overall YM mass-gap program:

Abstract curvature–FI link (Part I, Section 3).
The Bochner–Bakry–Émery identity and the 
𝐶

𝜌

Abstract interface to gauge theories (Part I, Section 5).
The Bakry–Émery tensor

These structures are then transported, via the OS reconstruction and comparison between configuration diffusion and Hamiltonian dynamics, into statements about finite-volume spectral gaps and candidate continuum mass gaps, as discussed in Parts IV–V.

3.6. Lyapunov functions and drift inequalities

In Yang–Mills and similar models one typically does not have a global curvature lower bound

4.4.3. Relation to physical mass

In a Hamiltonian framework, a physical mass gap typically refers to a lower bound on the spectrum of a quantum Hamiltonian 
𝐻
H above the ground state. The generator 
𝐿
L here is not that Hamiltonian, but the pattern is parallel:

a uniform lower bound on curvature,

implies a uniform spectral gap of a natural generator,

which controls correlation lengths and relaxation times.

The scalar model thus realizes, in a transparent way, the slogan

“Positive curvature (uniform convexity) ⇒ volume-independent spectral gap.”
